Codescent and bicolimits of pseudo-algebras
Axel Osmond

Abstract
We categorify cocompleteness results of monad theory, in the context of pseudomonads. We first prove a general result establishing that, in any 2-category, weighted bicolimits can be constructed from oplax bicolimits and bicoequalizers of codescent objects. After prerequisites on pseudomonads and their pseudo-algebras, we give a 2-dimensional Linton theorem reducing bicocompleteness of 2-categories of pseudo-algebras to existence of bicoequalizers of codescent objects. Finally we prove this condition to be fulfilled in the case of a bifinitary pseudomonad, ensuring bicocompleteness. Keywords. Codescent, bicolimits, pseudo-algebras, pseudomonad, bifinitary, transfinite induction, bicoequalizer of codescent object.



Introduction

This work provides a categorification of two classical results of monad theory. For a monad on a cocomplete category, it is well known that existence of colimits in the category of algebras reduces to existence of coequalizers, since the latter can be used in a first step to construct coproducts of algebras, and secondly, arbitrary colimits as quotients of such coproducts. In the case of a finitary monad (or more generally a monad with rank) such coequalizers can always be forced to exist thanks to a transfinite process, ensuring the cocompletness of categories of algebras of finitary monads.

In 2-category theory, 2-dimensional analogs of monads and their algebras decline in several flavours of strictness. As well as in the 1-categorical case, 2-categories of algebras of a 2-monad - whatever the notion of strictness one would consider - may lack some colimits without specific assumptions; however, in the case of
finitary
2-monads (or more generally for a 2-monad with rank) on a 2-complete and 2-cocomplete 2-category, it is known that both the 2-categories of
strict algebras
with either
strict morphisms
or
pseudomorphisms
are known to have all bicolimits: this result, originating in
[
BKP89
]
, first construct 2-colimits for strict morphisms, then uses strictification techniques to extract those bicolimits from existing stricter 2-colimits of strict algebras and strict morphisms through some adjunction.

However, this cannot be used for pseudo-algebras; it is established that not all pseudoalgebras of a 2-monad can be strictified into strict algebras (see for instance
[
Shu12
]
), even in the case of well-behaved 2-monad: this complicates any attempt to extract bicolimits from the 2-category of strict algebras and pseudomorphisms.

In this work, we consider the more general setting of
pseudomonads
, where the canonical natural transformations coding for the unit and the multiplication are related only through invertible natural modifications rather than strict equalities. Our motivation to consider pseudomonad rather than 2-monad originates in
[
DLO22
]
, where we prove that 2-categories of
\Phi
-exact
categories relative to a class of finite weights
\Phi
are finitely bipresentable: our proof implicitly requires bicocompletness of the 2-category of pseudo-algebras of a certain pseudomonad
[
GL12
]
associated to
\Phi
. In the context of pseudomonads, the stricter notions of algebras are less meaningful (for instance, the free construction does not return strict algebras): for this reason, it is far from clear whether strictification results of the kind of
[
BKP89
]
may help.

This paper proposes hence a “brute force” proof of the bicocompleteness of the 2-category of pseudo-algebras of bifinitary pseudomonads. Our strategy, inspired by the classics of 1-dimensional monad theory as
[
Bor94
]
[Section 4] or
[
BW00
]
, reduces existence of arbitrary bicolimits to existence of bicolimits of more specific shape, which can be more directly ensured, in particular for finitary pseudomonads.

In 1-dimension, it is known that cocompleteness of categories of algebras depends on the sole existence of coequalizers, and that those latter exist in the case of a finitary monad thanks to a famous, yet arcane strategy relying on a transfinite induction. In 2-dimension, though several shapes of bicolimits could provide generalizations of coequalizers of parallel pairs, we claim that in our context their correct analogs are
bicoequalizers of codescent objects
in the sense of
[
LCMV02
]
(also known as
codescent objects of coherence data
). Those latter encode additional data, which are akin to internal categories and also 2-dimensional notions of congruences. The role of codescent objects in the theory of 2-monads has been established for some time. Many results of monad theory involving instances of reflexives and split coequalizers generalize into pseudocoequalizing statements relative to some codescent objects in the 2-dimensional context. For instance, in
[
Bou10
]
and
[
LCMV02
]
, a 2-dimensional version of the
Barr construction
(also: the
resolution
or
reflexive coherence data
) of pseudo-algebras of pseudomonad is given, involving a canonical codescent object made of free pseudo-algebras.

In this paper, we prove that codescent objects are, more generally, useful to generate more general bicolimits. In 1-category theory, colimits can be constructed from coproducts and coequalizer; in 2-category theory, we often use the fact that weighted bicolimits can be constructed from coproduct, coinserted and coequifiers. We prove another result of this kind in our section 1: we reduce existence of weighted bicolimits to existence of oplax bicolimits and bicoequalizers of codescent objects, see
proposition

1.18
. Our argument relies on the more recent notion of
\sigma
-bicolimit
, intermediate between bicolimits and oplax bicolimits, which allows to turn weighted bicolimit into conical ones, see
proposition

1.2
. Then, for a given functor over a marked 2-category, we construct at
lemma

1.15
a certain codescent object from its oplax bicolimit, whose higher data encode the maps we are going to invert in the
\sigma
-bicolimit, and exhibit the
\sigma
-bicolimit as the bicoequalizer of this codescent diagram.

Then we apply this result in the context of pseudo-algebras of pseudomonads. We prove in section 3 that one can construct the oplax bicolimit of a diagram of pseudoalgebras as the bicoequalizer of a certain codescent diagram constructed from the oplax bicolimit of the objects underlying the algebras, see
theorem

3.14
, categorifying a famous result from
[
Lin69
]
. As a consequence, the sole existence of bicoequalizers of codescent objects in the 2-category of pseudo-algebras becomes sufficient to ensure existence of oplax bicolimits, and hence, following the result of our section 1, of all bicolimit.

Finally, in section 4, we establish the existence of bicoequalizers of codescent diagrams in the 2-category of pseudo-algebras for a bifinitary pseudomonad – see
corollary

4.21
. Our construction is very close in spirit to
[
Bor94
]
[Theorem 4.3.6]. This ensures from what precedes that the 2-category of pseudo-algebras of a bifinitary pseudomonad is always bicocomplete, which is
theorem

4.22
. From this we deduce cocompleteness of various examples as
{\bf Lex}
and 2-categories of
\Phi
-exact categories for classes of finite weights.

1
An observation on oplax bicolimits and codescent objects

First recall that bicolimits in
{\bf Cat}
are pseudocolimits. Moreover, conical bicolimits are computed as localizations of oplax-colimits at cartesian morphisms. We would like to extend this result to arbitrary weighted bicolimits. We recall here the notion of
\sigma
-bicolimit
(also
marked bicolimit
in
[
GHL21
]
), the main reference for it being
[
DDS18
]

Definition 1.1

(
\sigma
-natural transformations)
.

Let be
I
a 2-category and
\Sigma
a class of maps in
I
containing equivalences and closed under composition and invertible 2-cells; let be
\mathcal{C}
a category and
F,G:I\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
a pair of 2-functors. A
\sigma
-
natural transformation
relatively to
\Sigma
is a lax natural transformation
f:F\Rightarrow G
whose lax naturality squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{s}}

at an arrow
s
in
\Sigma
are invertible 2-cells of
\mathcal{C}
. Similarly an
op
\sigma
-
natural transformation
is an oplax natural transformation whose oplax naturality squares at arrow at arrows in
\Sigma
are invertible. We denote as
[I,\mathcal{C}]_{\Sigma}
the 2-category of strict 2-functors and
\sigma
-natural transformations relatively to
\Sigma
, with no restriction on 2-cells, and
[I,\mathcal{C}]_{{\rm op}\Sigma}
for op
\sigma
-natural transformations.

A
\sigma
-cocone
is an
op
\sigma
-natural transformation
q:F\Rightarrow\Delta_{B}
, with oplax naturality triangles - with the
q_{s}
for
s\in\Sigma
invertible:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{d}}

Now the
\sigma
-bicolimit
of a 2-functor
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
relative to
\Sigma
is a
\sigma
-cocone
q:F\Rightarrow\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}_{I}\,F
such that one has a natural equivalence

\mathcal{C}[\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F(i),-]\simeq[I,\mathcal{C}]_{{\rm op}\Sigma}[F,\Delta]

The interest of
\sigma
-bicolimit is that they allow to “deweight” weighted bicolimits (see more generally
[
DDS18
]
[2.4.9]):

Proposition 1.2

(Deweighting lemma)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a 2-category. If
\mathcal{C}
has (conical)
\sigma
-bicolimits, then it has all weighted bicolimits.

Proof.

Let be
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
with
I
a small 2-category and
W:I^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
a 2-functor. One can take the 2-category of elements
\int W
and consider the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\int W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

The 2-category of elements
\int W
is endowed with the class of cartesian arrows
{\bf Cart}_{W}
: we claim that the weighted bicolimit of
F
can be recovered as the
\sigma
-bicolimit of
F\pi_{W}
at the cartesian arrows, that is

\underset{I}{{\textup{bicolim}}^{W}}\;F\simeq{\bf Cart}_{W}\underset{I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;F\pi_{W}

∎

1.3
.

In
{\bf Cat}
,
\sigma
-bicolimits are
\sigma
-pseudocolimits and can be constructed in the same way as pseudocolimits by localizing oplax bicolimits, but this time only at cartesian arrows over arrows in the marked class. More precisely, for a
\sigma
- pair
(I,\Sigma)
and a 2-functor
F:I\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
, the
\sigma
-colimit is obtained as the localization of the oplax colimit at cartesian lifts of
\Sigma
-arrows. If one defines

\Sigma_{(F,\Sigma)}=\textbf{Cart}_{F}\cap\pi_{F}^{-1}(\Sigma)

with
\pi_{F}:{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{i\in I}F(i)\rightarrow I
the associated fibration (recall that
{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{i\in I}F(i)
is the underlying category of the Grothendieck construction of
F
), one has the equation below. Moreover this
\sigma
-bicolimit can be chosen as a
\sigma
-bicolimit.

\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F(i)\simeq\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{oplaxcolim}}}\,F(i)[\Sigma_{(F,\Sigma)}^{-1}].

We want to generalize this statement in arbitrary 2-categories: this implies to understand what a correct generalization of localizations is. While this operation could be described in term of coinverters and coequifiers, we shall be interested in a special class of bicolimits, which could really be seen as a colimit of internal categories - here encoding the arrows one wants to localize as if they lived
inside
an object. The following notion will be central to our work; we follow here mostly defintions and notations from
[
LCMV02
]
.

1.4
Codescent diagram.

In the following
\mathbb{X}
will denote the following truncated simplicial object

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}

where we shall denote the
d_{0},i,d_{1}
as the
lower codescent data
and the
p_{0},p_{1},p_{2}
as the
higher codescent data
, together with the following invertible 2-cells exhibiting
i
as a common pseudosection of
d_{0},d_{1}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{0}\atop\simeq}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{1}\atop\simeq}

and the following invertible 2-cells:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{01}\atop\simeq}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{02}\atop\simeq}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{12}\atop\simeq}

Definition 1.5
.

A
codescent object
in a 2-category
\mathcal{C}
is a 2-functor
\mathscr{X}:\mathbb{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. A
morphism of codescent object
is a pseudonatural transformation in
[\mathbb{X},\mathcal{C}]_{\bf ps}
.

Codescent diagrams define shapes over which we can compute the following kind of weighted bicolimit, their
bicoequalizer
:

Definition 1.6
.

We shall say that a morphism
q:\mathscr{X}(0)\rightarrow C

pseudocoequalizes

\mathbb{X}
if it inserts an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{X}(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{X}(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{X}(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}(d_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}(d_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\atop\simeq}

satisfying moreover the following identity, which we shall refer to as the
lower coherence condition
:

\hbox to188.67pt{\vbox to106.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 94.33344pt\lower-53.19997pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

A
bicoequalizer
of a codescent object
\mathscr{X}
is the data of a pair
(q_{\mathscr{X}}:\mathscr{X}(0)\rightarrow{\textup{bicoeq}}(\mathscr{X}),\xi_{\mathscr{X}})
which is universal amongst the one pseudocoequalizing
\mathscr{X}
, in the sense that for any other pseudocoequalizing
(C,q,\xi)
there is a canonical 1-cell
\langle q,\xi\rangle:{\textup{bicoeq}}(\mathscr{X})\rightarrow C
, unique up to unique invertible 2-cell, together with a universal 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{X}(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicoeq}}(\mathscr{X})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\mathscr{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q,\xi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{\langle q,\xi\rangle}\atop\simeq}

satisfying the following identity - natural in
(C,q,\xi)
:

\hbox to204.31pt{\vbox to105.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 104.79431pt\lower-51.39584pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Remark 1.7
.

The most formal definition of the bicoequalizer, one can find in
[
LCMV02
]
[Definition 2.1], involves a convenient weight and describe the bicoequalizer as a weighted bicolimit, which is more suited to correctly state the universal property. Since we are going to make a brief reference to it later, we give a quick expression of it: it is the 2-functor
\mathcal{J}:\mathbb{X}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
defined as
\mathcal{J}(0)=*
(the point category),
\mathcal{J}(1)=\{x\simeq y\}
(the walking isomorphism) and
\mathcal{J}(2)=\{x\simeq y\simeq z\}
(the walking triangle of isomorphisms);
\mathcal{J}(d_{0}),\mathcal{J}(d_{1}):\mathcal{J}(0)\rightrightarrows\mathcal{J}(1)
point respectively to
x
and
y
, and
\mathcal{J}(p_{0}),\mathcal{J}(p_{1}),\mathcal{J}(p_{2})
point respectively toward
x\simeq y
,
x\simeq z
,
y\simeq z
.

Remark 1.8
.

The terminology in the theory of codescent is not totally unified. Here we follow
[
LCMV02
]
terminology. Another terminology is also widely accepted:
[
Bou10
]
(see for instance section 2.2) as well as
[
Lac02
]
speak of
coherence data
for what we call a codescent object and say codescent object for our bicoequalizers.

Remark 1.9
.

[Pseudofunctoriality of bicoequalizers of codescent objects]
As any bicolimit construction, taking bicoequalizers defines a pseudofunctor
{\textup{bicoeq}}(-):[\mathbb{X},\mathcal{C}]\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. This means that any morphism of codescent objects
x:\mathscr{X}\Rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
defines uniquely a canonical invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{X}(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicoeq}}{(\mathscr{X})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{Y}(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicoeq}}{(\mathscr{Y})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\mathscr{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{bicoeq}}{(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\mathscr{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{x}\atop\simeq}

satisfying the coherence condition

Remark 1.10
.

To any morphism
f:A\rightarrow B
in a 2-category
\mathcal{C}
with finite bilimits we can associate a codescent object, its nerve:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\simeq f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f}\atop\simeq}

In particular the identity of
A
induces trivially a common pseudosection of
d_{0},d_{1}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\simeq f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Those data define a simplicial object which can be shown to be codescent

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\simeq f\simeq f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\simeq f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

In particular, in
{\bf Cat}
, given a localization
q_{\Sigma}:C\rightarrow C[\Sigma^{-1}]
, one can consider the corresponding codescent object

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{q_{F}\simeq q_{F}\simeq q_{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{q_{F}\simeq q_{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}

Then
q_{F}\simeq q_{F}
can be seen as indexing arrows of
\Sigma
(up to an equivalence) with
d_{0}
,
d_{1}
the restricted domain and codomain, while
\iota
represents the fact that all identities are in particular isomorphisms, hence are in particular inverted by
q_{F}
. The object
q_{F}\simeq q_{F}\simeq q_{F}
is equivalent to the object
\Sigma\times_{C}\Sigma
of composable pairs and expresses that
\Sigma
is to be closed under composition.

Remark 1.11
.

In particular, applying this construction in
{\bf Cat}
to the localization of an oplax pseudocolimit to the corresponding pseudocolimit

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{oplaxpscolim}}}\;F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{pscolim}}}\;F(i)\simeq\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{oplaxpscolim}}}\;F(i)[{\bf Cart}_{F}^{-1}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{F}}

we get an oplax codescent object whose pseudocoequalizer coincides with the localization
q_{F}:C\rightarrow C[\Sigma^{-1}]
. Moreover, because of the coherence condition in the oplax 2-cell at any 2-cell
\sigma:d\Rightarrow d^{\prime}:i\rightarrow j
in
I
given by
q_{d^{\prime}}q_{j}*F(\sigma)=q_{d}
we know that the
F(\sigma)
are also inverted by the localization
q_{F}
because the class of inverted maps generated from
{\bf Cart}_{F}
satisfies the 2 out of 3 axiom and
q_{d},q_{d^{\prime}}
are inverted.

However here we used an already existing bicolimit to construct this codescent object: in this paper, we shall rather construct a bicolimit from oplax bicolimit and bicoequalizers of codescent objects. We want to show that this is a totally general and normal process. We propose here a quite natural categorification of the classical process of constructing colimits from coproducts and coequalizers.

This remark generalizes for the case of a
\sigma
-colimit in
{\bf Cat}
for it is obtained also as a localization: for any diagram
F:I\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
and
\Sigma
a class of maps in
I
, one can compute as above a codescent object at the localization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{oplaxpscolim}}}\;F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{pscolim}}}\;F(i)\simeq\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{oplaxpscolim}}}\;F(i)[\Sigma_{(F,\Sigma)}^{-1}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{F}}

where
\Sigma_{(F,\Sigma)}
denotes the class of cartesian lifts of maps in
\Sigma
as in
1.3
.

1.12
Codescent diagram associated to a marked 2-category.

It is well known that any 2-category induces a 2-truncated simplicial object called its nerve, which is in fact a codescent object. Here we describe the same construction yet with a restriction at the level of arrows to encode a choice of a class as in a
\sigma
-bicolimit.

Let be
\mathcal{C}
a 2-category,
I
a small 2-category,
\Sigma
a distinguished class of arrows of
I
containing identities and stable under composition. First, denote as
\Sigma\hookrightarrow I
the (0,2)-full subcategory of
I
consisting of all objects and only 1-cells in
\Sigma
with all 2-cells between them. In the following we want a category correctly indexing those arrows that are in
\Sigma
but seen as objects. To do so, consider first the arrow 2-category
[2,I]_{{\bf ps}}
of 2-functors
2\rightarrow I
from the walking arrow, together with pseudonatural transformations between them (so that morphisms in
[2,I]_{{\bf ps}}
correspond to pseudosquares). Define now the following (1,2)-full sub-2-category
\Sigma^{2}\hookrightarrow[2,I]_{{\bf ps}}
consisting only of those arrows that lay in
\Sigma
. Beware that its morphisms are all pseudosquares of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm dom}\,d_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm dom}\,d_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}\,d_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}\,d_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\atop\simeq}

where
\phi_{0},\phi_{1}
are
not
required to live in
\Sigma
- though
d_{0},d_{1}
are. Its two cells, which are modification of 2-functors, correspond to morphisms of pseudosquares.

This 2-category is equipped with a parallel pair
{\rm dom},{\rm cod}:\Sigma^{2}\rightrightarrows I
. Moreover, because
\Sigma
is stipulated as containing identities, the identity morphism
{\rm id}:I\rightarrow I^{2}
admits a factorization
I\rightarrow\Sigma^{2}
. This factorization provides a pseudosection of both
{\rm dom}
and
{\rm cod}
.

Now the higher data. Let
3
denote the walking invertible triangular 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\atop\simeq}

We can consider the 2-functor 2-category
[3,I]_{{\bf ps}}
, and take again its (1,2)-full sub-2-category
\Sigma^{3}
consisting of all triangular 2-cells in
I
whose edges are in
\Sigma
- while morphisms are given by the pseudonatural transformations and 2-cells as modifications. Then we are provided with a canonical codescent object associated to the choice of
\Sigma

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm dom}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm id}}

Observe that this codescent object is moreover strict in the sense that the invertible 2-cells in its codescent data are actually equalities
{\rm dom}\,{\rm id}=1_{I}={\rm cod}\,{\rm id}
,
{\rm dom}\,p_{0}={\rm dom}\,p_{1}
,
{\rm cod}\,p_{0}={\rm dom}\,p_{2}
and
{\rm cod}\,p_{1}={\rm cod}\,p_{2}
. We are now going to use this codescent diagram to index a canonical codescent diagram over any oplax bicolimit given a choice of marked maps.

1.13
Codescent data associated to a
\sigma
-bicolimit: 1-dimensional data.

Let be
\mathcal{C}
a 2-category,
I
a small 2-category,
\Sigma
a distinguished class of arrows of
I
containing identities and stable under composition, and
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
a 2-functor. Suppose that oplax bicolimits exist in
\mathcal{C}
and take the oplax bicolimit
(q_{i}:F(i)\rightarrow{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}_{I}F(i))_{i\in I}
with
q_{d}:q_{j}F(d)\Rightarrow q_{i}
the oplax transition 2-cell at an arrow
i\in I
: the latter correspond to the cartesian arrows in the
{\bf Cat}
-valued case - and we want precisely to formally inverse them. To this purpose, we construct a codescent object formally indexing the oplax 2-cells as follows.

Consider the whiskering

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm dom}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}}

and take the oplax bicolimit of the composite
F{\rm dom}:\Sigma^{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
; then in particular for each
d
in
\Sigma^{2}
we have both an inclusion at
{\rm dom}(d)
and another one in
{\rm cod}(d)
related by the oplax 2-cell at
d
. In fact this provides us with two distinct oplax cocones over
F{\rm dom}
toward the oplax bicolimit
{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}_{I}F
:

-

the one provided by the data of all the
(q_{{\rm dom}(d)}:F{\rm dom}(d)\rightarrow{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}_{I}F)_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}
, where the transition 2-cells are given as follows: for a 1-cell
\phi:d_{0}\rightarrow d_{1}
in
\Sigma^{2}
, that is, for a pseudosquare

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm dom}(d_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(d_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm dom}(d_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(d_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\atop\simeq}

just take the oplax 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({\rm dom}(d_{0}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({\rm dom}(d_{1}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\phi_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{{\rm dom}(d_{0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{{\rm dom}(d_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\phi_{0}}}

and for a 2-cell in
\Sigma^{2}
, that is, a natural modification
\lambda:\phi\Rrightarrow\psi
corresponding to an equality of 2-cells

\hbox to132.79pt{\vbox to56.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 67.62032pt\lower-28.02637pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\sigma

\Sigma

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\atop\simeq}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{3}}}

Lemma 1.15
.

The following data define a codescent object
\mathscr{X}_{F,\Sigma}:\mathbb{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{3}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}\,p_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)}F(d)\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}\,{\rm id}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{0}\phi}\rangle_{\phi\in\Sigma^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{2}\phi}F(p_{0}\phi)\rangle_{\phi\in\Sigma^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{1}\phi}\rangle_{\phi\in\Sigma^{3}}}

Proof.

The pseudosection 2-cells corresponding to the
n_{0}
and
n_{1}
of
1.4
are induced from the universal property of the oplax bicolimit from the existence in each
i
of the following decomposition of the inclisions - using that
i={\rm dom}\,{\rm id}_{i}={\rm cod}\,{\rm id}_{i}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)=F{\rm dom}\,({\rm id}_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{{\rm id}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{{\rm dom}\,{\rm id}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{{\rm id}_{i}}\atop\simeq}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)=F{\rm dom}\,({\rm id}_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)=F{\rm cod}\,({\rm id}_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{{\rm id}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{{\rm cod}\,{\rm id}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(i)={\rm id}_{F(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{{\rm id}_{i}}\atop\simeq}

For the higher condition: we have at each
\phi
of
\Sigma^{3}
an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F{\rm dom}\,p_{0}\phi=F{\rm dom}\,p_{1}\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{0}\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{1}\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{{\rm dom}\,p_{0}\phi}=q_{{\rm dom}\,p_{1}\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime-1}_{p_{1}\phi}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{p_{0}\phi}\atop\simeq}

which induces, by the universal property of the oplaxbicolimit, the desired invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{3}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}\,p_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{0}\phi}\rangle_{\phi\in\Sigma^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{1}\phi}\rangle_{\phi\in\Sigma^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{01}\atop\simeq}

Similarly the middle 2-cell
\theta_{02}
is induced from the pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F{\rm dom}\,p_{0}\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F{\rm cod}\,p_{0}\phi=F{\rm dom}\,p_{2}\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fp_{0}\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{0}\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{2}\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)F(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{{\rm dom}\,p_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{p_{2}\phi}^{-1}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{p_{0}\phi}\atop\simeq}

Finally the last invertible 2-cell
\theta_{12}
is induced from the pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F{\rm dom}\,p_{0}\phi=F{\rm dom}\,p_{1}\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F{\rm cod}\,p_{0}\phi=F{\rm dom}\,p_{2}\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F{\rm cod}\,p_{1}\phi=F{\rm cod}\,p_{2}\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fp_{0}\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{1}\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{2}\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)F(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)F(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fp_{1}\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fp_{2}\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{{\rm cod}\,p_{1}\phi}=q_{{\rm cod}\,p_{2}\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\phi\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{p_{0}\phi}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime-1}_{p_{2}\phi}\atop\simeq}

∎

Remark 1.16
.

In the following we are going to compare the bicolimit of the diagram
F
and the bicoequalizer of the codescent object
\mathscr{X}_{F,\Sigma}
. Before embarking in this task, we should compare our construction with the canonical codescent object constructed at the localization
q_{F,\Sigma}:{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{I}F\rightarrow\Sigma{\textup{pscolim}}_{I}F
in the case of a diagram valuated in
{\bf Cat}
in order to check this construction subsumes the canonical one. Take
F:I\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
and
\Sigma
in
I^{2}
. Let us compare the data of
\mathscr{X}_{F,\Sigma}
with the canonical codescent object at
q_{F,\Sigma}
defined at
remark

1.11
.

The object
q_{F,\Sigma}\simeq q_{F,\Sigma}
, being the pseudopullback of
q_{F,\Sigma}
at itself, can be described concretely as follows. Its objects are data
((i,a),(j,b),(d,\alpha))
with
a
an object of
F(i)
,
b
an object of
F(j)
,
s:i\rightarrow j
a morphism in
\Sigma
and
\alpha:F(d)(a)\simeq b
an isomorphism in
F(j)
, so that the pair
(d,\alpha)
becomes an isomorphism in
\Sigma{\textup{colim}}_{I}F
as depicted below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma\underset{I}{{\textup{colim}}}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{d}\atop\simeq}

A morphism
((i_{0},a_{0}),(j_{0},b_{0},(s_{0},\alpha_{0}))\rightarrow((i_{1},a_{1}),(j_{1},b_{1},(s_{1},\alpha_{1}))
consists of a pair of morphisms
(\phi,u):(i_{0},a_{0})\rightarrow(i_{1},a_{1})
,
(\psi,v):(j_{0},b_{0})\rightarrow(j_{1},b_{1})
in
{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{I}F
ensuring the following commutation in the
\sigma
-colimit (where, beware,
\phi
and
\psi
are arbitrary arrows of
I
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{0},a_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{0},b_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{1},a_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{1},b_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{0},\alpha_{0})\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi,u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\psi,v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{1},\alpha_{1})\atop\simeq}

Hence we have a functor
{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{\Sigma^{2}}F{\rm dom}\rightarrow q_{F,\Sigma}\simeq q_{F,\Sigma}
sending
(d,a)
(with
d
in
\Sigma
) to the data
({\rm dom}\,d,a),({\rm cod}\,d,F(d)(a)),(d,1_{F(d)(a)}))
and a morphism
(\phi,u):(d_{0},a_{0})\rightarrow(d_{1},a_{1})
(with
\phi
a pseudosquare and
u:a_{1}\rightarrow F(\phi_{0})(a_{0})
) to the pair
(\phi_{0},u),(\phi_{1},\phi F(d_{1})(u))
. Conversely, any data
((i,a),(j,b),(d,\alpha))
, for
d:i\rightarrow j
has to be in
\Sigma
, can be sent to the pair
(d,a)
in
{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{\Sigma^{2}}F{\rm dom}
, while a morphism
(\phi,u),(\psi,v)
to the pair consisting of the strict underlying pseudosquare
d_{1}\phi=\psi d_{0}
together with the arrow
u
in
F({\rm dom}\,d_{1})
.

Those two functors can be shown to define an equivalence: indeed one can trivially recover any
(d,a)
in
{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{\Sigma^{2}}F{\rm dom}
, from
({\rm dom}\,d,a),({\rm cod}\,d,F(d)(a)),(d,1_{F(d)(a)}))
up to equality, while on the other side, one has a canonical isomorphism
((1_{i},1_{a}),(1_{j},\alpha)):((i,a),(j,b),(d,\alpha))\simeq((i,a),(j,F(a)),(d,1_{F(a)}))
in
q_{F,\Sigma}\simeq q_{F,\Sigma}
. This ensures the existence of a retract equivalence

{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{\Sigma^{2}}F{\rm dom}\simeq(q_{F,\Sigma}\simeq q_{F,\Sigma})

Similarly, one can check that the higher data are also equivalent, that is,
{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{\Sigma^{3}}F{\rm dom}\,p_{0}
is equivalent to
q_{F,\Sigma}\simeq q_{F,\Sigma}\simeq q_{F,\Sigma}
. The induced morphisms between the higher data are also expected to coincide, which may be shown using the universal properties of the different objects involved here. Hence, being equivalent, the two codescent objects considered above will have the same pseudocoequalizers up to canonical equivalences, which comfort us in the intuition that the pseudocoequalizer of the codescent diagram
\mathscr{X}_{F,\Sigma}
has to be the
\sigma
-colimit for it will coincide with the convenient localization in
{\bf Cat}
. We are going to prove directly why it is so in an arbitrary 2-category.

1.17
.

We now get back to the general setting, with
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
a diagram and
\Sigma
a class of maps in
I
. Whenever it exists, the bicoequalizer is equipped with an oplax cocone over
F
given by the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicoeq}}(\mathscr{X}_{F,\Sigma})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{F,\Sigma}}

together with the whiskering of oplax 2-cells
q_{F,\Sigma}*q_{d}
at each
d
in
I^{2}
. Moreover the bicoequalizer inserts a canonical invertible 2-cell satisfying the coherence conditions of
definition

1.6
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicoeq}}(\mathscr{X}_{F,\Sigma})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in I^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{F,\Sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)}F(d)\rangle_{d\in I^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{F,\Sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{F,\Sigma}\atop\simeq}

Proposition 1.18
.

Whenever it exists, the bicoequalizer of
\mathscr{X}_{F,\Sigma}
is a
\sigma
-bicolimit of
{F}
:

{\textup{bicoeq}}(\mathscr{X}_{F,\Sigma})\simeq\Sigma\underset{I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;F

Proof.

Recall that the bicoequalizer of a codescent object was defined as being universal amongst those that pseudocoequalizes the codescent object as in
definition

1.6
. We prove here that, for a given object
C
of
\mathcal{C}
, the category of pairs
(q:{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}_{I}F\rightarrow C,\xi)
pseudocoequalizig the codescent object
\mathscr{X}_{F,\Sigma}
and the category of
\Sigma
-cocones over
F
with tip
C
are pseudonaturally equivalent.

Suppose we have a 1-cell
q:{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}_{I}F\rightarrow C
together with an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in I^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)}F(d)\rangle_{d\in I^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\atop\simeq}

satisfying moreover the required coherence conditions. Then it induces an oplax cocone
F\Rightarrow C
with component
qq_{i}:F(i)\rightarrow C
at
i
and the whiskering
q*q_{d}
at
d
in
I^{2}
. But now, in the case where
d
is in
\Sigma
, the whiskering above happens to coincide with the following pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F{\rm dom}(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F{\rm cod}(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{{\rm cod}(d)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)}F(d)\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in I^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{{\rm dom}(d)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{d}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{d}\atop\simeq}

which is an invertible 2-cell: this forces the oplax transition 2-cells of the induced oplax cocone to be invertible, hence this cocone to be actually a
\sigma
-cocone relative to
\Sigma
.

Conversely, if one has a
\Sigma
-cocone
(q^{\prime}_{i}:F(i)\rightarrow C)_{i\in I}
; it is in particular a oplax cocone, for which it admits a universal factorization through
{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}_{I}F

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{i}\atop\simeq}

Now for each distinguished arrow
d
of
\Sigma
we have a decomposition of the oplax 2-cell
q^{\prime}_{d}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({{\rm dom}(d)})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({{\rm cod}(d)})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{{\rm dom}(d)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{{\rm dom}(d)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{{\rm cod}(d)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{{\rm cod}(d)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{{\rm dom}(d)}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{{\rm cod}(d)}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{d}}

But for
q^{\prime}_{d}
is invertible as
d
is in
\Sigma
, the decomposition above forces by cancellation of invertible 2-cells the whiskering
\langle q^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}*\theta_{d}q_{d}\theta^{\prime-1}_{d}
to be itself invertible. But then by universal property of the oplax bicolimit, this invertible 2-cell induces uniquely an invertible 2-cell
\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)}F(d)\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}
with the property that for each
d
in
\Sigma
we have an equality of whiskerings:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({{\rm dom}(d)})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)}F(d)\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{d}q_{d}\theta^{\prime-1}_{d}}

We must show this inserted 2-cell satisfies the coherence conditions. For the lower condition, we use pseudofunctoriality of the oplax bicolimit construction. Observe that the identity of
\langle q^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}
is jointly induced by the identities
1_{\langle q^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}}*q_{i}:\langle q^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}q_{i}=\langle q^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}q_{i}
. But if we consider the whiskering of the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}\,{\rm id}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)}F(d)\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\theta_{d}q_{d}\theta^{\prime-1}_{d}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\theta^{\rm dom}_{{\rm id}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\theta^{\rm cod}_{{\rm id}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in I}}

with each of the projections
q_{i}
we get the pasting below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{{\rm dom}\,{\rm id}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)}F(d)\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\theta_{d}q_{d}\theta^{\prime-1}_{d}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\rm dom}_{{\rm id}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\rm cod}_{{\rm id}_{i}}}

But from the way we constructed the inserted 2-cell this pasting recomposes as the following

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)}F(d)\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{{\rm id}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{{\rm id}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)}F(d)\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{{\rm id}_{i}}q_{{\rm id}_{i}}\theta^{\prime-1}_{{\rm id}_{i}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{{\rm id}_{i}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{-1}_{{\rm id}_{i}}\atop\simeq}

But now pasting the isomorphisms
\theta_{{\rm id}_{i}}
and
\theta_{{\rm id}_{i}}^{\prime}
with their respective inverses return identity 2-cells, while the oplax transition 2-cells
q_{{\rm id}_{i}}
always are identities: hence the pasting above really reduces on the identity, and induces the identity
\langle q^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}
by passing through the bicolimit.

Now we must prove that the inserted 2-cell
\langle\theta_{d}q_{d}\theta^{\prime-1}_{d}\rangle
satisfies the higher coherence condition. Consider the following pasting of the inserted 2-cell along the outer higher data:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{3}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}\,p_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{1}\phi}\rangle_{\phi\in\Sigma^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)F(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{0}\phi}\rangle_{\phi\in\Sigma^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\theta_{d}q_{d}\theta^{\prime-1}_{d}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)F(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{2}\phi}F(p_{0}\phi)\rangle_{\phi\in\Sigma^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{12}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{01}\atop\simeq}

We are again going to prove that this pasting is locally equal to the other one: we saw at
lemma

1.15
how the higher coherence data were induced from a family of invertible 2-cells at each
\phi
of
\Sigma^{3}
; then it suffices to replace them in the diagram above and reduce it to a pasting of
\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)F(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}
along the 2-cell at
\phi
from the family from which we induced the intermediate coherence data. Consider the following pasting:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F{\rm cod}\,p_{0}\phi=F{\rm dom}\,p_{2}\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F{\rm cod}\,p_{1}\phi=F{\rm cod}\,p_{2}\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F{\rm dom}\,p_{0}\phi=F{\rm dom}\,p_{1}\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fp_{0}\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{1}\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{2}\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)F(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fp_{1}\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{{\rm cod}\,p_{1}\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{0}\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\theta_{d}q_{d}\theta^{\prime-1}_{d}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)F(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{p_{0}\phi}\theta^{-1}_{p_{1}\phi}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fp_{2}\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{p_{1}\phi}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime-1}_{p_{2}\phi}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\phi\atop\simeq}

Then we know that the inserted 2-cell, from the very universal property it was induced from in the first part of this proof, satisfies the whiskering equality

\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)F(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}*q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{1}\phi}=\langle q^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}*\theta_{p_{1}\phi}q_{p_{1}\phi}\theta^{\prime-1}_{p_{1}\phi}

(where, beware, the oplax transition 2-cell
q_{p_{1}\phi}
is not invertible) so the diagram above decomposes as the following pasting where
\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)F(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}
has been whiskered along

:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F{\rm cod}\,p_{0}\phi=F{\rm dom}\,p_{2}\phi}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\phi\atop\simeq}

Now we can compose in this diagram the universal invertible 2-cell

in order to replace the outer coherence 2-cells we started with two copies of the coequalizing 2-cell. We then obtain the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}

Applying again the whiskering identity yields the following desired pasting with the intermediate coherence 2-cell (as it was defined at

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{p_{0}\phi}\atop\simeq}

But the intermediate 2-cell is the one from which we induced the intermediate coherence data in the codescent diagram through the universal property of the oplax bicolimit, which provides the equality with the desired pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma^{2}}{{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}}\;F{\rm dom}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm cod}(d)F(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{{\rm dom}(d)}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\theta_{d}q_{d}\theta^{\prime-1}_{d}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\theta_{d}q_{d}\theta^{\prime-1}_{d}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{2}\phi}F(p_{0}\phi)\rangle_{\phi\in\Sigma^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q^{{\rm dom}}_{p_{0}\phi}\rangle_{\phi\in\Sigma^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{02}\atop\simeq}

Hence the induced 1-cell
\langle q^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}
together with its universal 2-cell
\langle\theta_{d}q_{d}\theta^{\prime-1}_{d}\rangle_{d\in\Sigma^{2}}
satisfies the higher coherence data, which achieves to prove it pseudocoequalizes the codescent diagram
\mathscr{X}_{F,\Sigma}
.

To sum up, we proved that any pair pseudocoequalizing the codescent diagram
\mathscr{X}_{F,\Sigma}
defines uniquely a
\sigma
-cocone over
F
relative to
\Sigma
, and that conversely any such
\sigma
-cocone defines a pseudocoequalizing pair. It is clear from our process that those constructions are uniquely defined and induce, for any object
C
, equivalences between
\sigma
-cocones over
F
for
\Sigma
with tip
C
and pairs
(q:{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}_{I}F\rightarrow C,\xi)
pseudocoequalizing
\mathscr{X}_{F,\Sigma}
. Hence the 2-functors sending
C
on those categories are naturally equivalent, and hence, if one is representable, so is the other one and by the same objects. This achieves to prove that a
\sigma
-bicolimit of
F
for
\Sigma
is the same as a bicoequalizer of
\mathscr{X}_{F,\Sigma}
.
∎

Theorem 1.19
.

Let be
\mathcal{C}
a 2-category. Then
\mathcal{C}
is bicocomplete if and only if it has oplax bicolimits and bicoequalizers of codescent objects.

Proof.

We saw at
proposition

1.2
that any weighted bicolimit can be obtained as a conical
\sigma
-bicolimit. But from
proposition

1.18
, any conical
\sigma
-bicolimit can be obtained as the bicoequalizer of a codescent diagram constructed from oplax bicolimits.
∎

This result will be now used in the third and fourth sections of this paper where we shall reduce a bicocompleteness result to existence of bicoequalizers of codescent objects.

2
Pseudomonads, pseudo-algebras and codescent

Here we recall some elements of pseudomonad theory. In particular we recall the 2-dimensional bar construction of
[
LCMV02
]
. An important source for pseudomonad is also
[
Nun17
]
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
pseudomonad
on a 2-category
\mathcal{C}
is a 2-functor
T:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
equipped with two pseudonatural transformations
unit

\eta:1\Rightarrow T
and a
multiplication

\mu:TT\Rightarrow T
together with canonical invertible 2-cells
(\xi,\zeta)
and
\rho
defined as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta\atop\simeq}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho\atop\simeq}

Definition 2.2
.

A
pseudo-algebra
of a pseudomonad
(T,\eta,\mu,(\xi,\zeta,\rho))
is a pair
(A,a,(\alpha^{t},\alpha^{s}))
with
A
an object of
\mathcal{C}
and
a:A\rightarrow TA
a 1-cell in
\mathcal{C}
and
(\alpha^{t},\alpha^{s})
is a pair to 2-cells as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{t}\atop\simeq}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{s}\atop\simeq}

subject to the following coherence conditions:

Definition 2.3
.

Let
(T,\eta,\mu,(\xi,\zeta,\rho))
be a pseudomonad and
(A,a,(\alpha^{t},\alpha^{s}))
,
(B,b,(\beta^{t},\beta^{s}))
two pseudo-algebras: then a
pseudomorphism

(A,a,(\alpha^{t},\alpha^{s}))\rightarrow(B,b,(\beta^{t},\beta^{s}))
is a pair
(f,\phi)
with an arrow
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathcal{C}
and
\phi
an invertible 2-cell as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

subject to the following coherence conditions:

\hbox to105.74pt{\vbox to85.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 52.86977pt\lower-43.79721pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Definition 2.4
.

Let
(f,\phi),(g,\gamma):(A,a,(\alpha^{t},\alpha^{s}))\rightrightarrows(B,b,(\beta^{t},\beta^{s}))
be two pseudomorphisms of pseudo-algebras with same domain and codomain; then a transformation between them is a 2-cell
\alpha:f\rightarrow g
in
\mathcal{C}
such that

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

This defines a 2-category
T\mbox{-}{\bf psAlg}
, whose 0-cells are pseudo-algebras, 1-cells are pseudomorphisms of T-pseudoa-algebras, and 2-cells are transformations between them.

2.5
.

For a pseudomonad
(T,\eta,\mu,(\xi,\zeta,\rho))
, we have then a forgetful functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T\mbox{-}{\bf psAlg}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{T}}

sending an algebra
(A,a,(\alpha^{t},\alpha^{s}))
on the underlying
A
and
(f,\phi)
on
f
. This functor is right pseudo-adjoint to the associated free functor sending
A
to the pseudo-algebra
(TA,\mu_{A},(\xi_{A},\rho_{A}))
and
f
to
(f,\mu_{f})
with
\mu_{f}
the naturality square of
\mu
at
f
: it is standard calculation so see that we have a pseudo-adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T\mbox{-}{\bf psAlg}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{T}}

In
[
Osm21
]
we proved a pseudo-version of
[
BKP89
]
[Theorem 2.6]:

Proposition 2.6
.

[
[
Osm21
]
proposition 6.3.1.3]
Let be a pseudomonad on a 2-category with bilimits. Then the forgetful functor creates bilimits.

However, in this work we are interested in bicolimits of pseudoalgebras, which will be the topic of the next two sections. It is known that not all 2-category of pseudo-algebras of a pseudomonad - neither their stricter or lower dimensional analogs - is bicocomplete in general: some additional conditions must be enforced. Several kinds of conditions are known to enable computation of bicolimits of pseudo-algebras, for instance preservation of bicolimits of a certain shape by the 2-functor
T
:

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
(T,\eta,\mu,(\xi,\zeta,\rho))
be a pseudomonad on a 2-category
\mathcal{C}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
has
I
-indexed conical bicolimits of
I
a 2-category and that
T
preserves them. Then
T\mbox{-}{\bf psAlg}
has
I
-indexed conical bicolimits and
U_{T}
creates them.

However, in the following, we are interested in establishing the existence of arbitrary bicolimits, without assumption about their preservation by
T
. This will rely on auxiliary results on codescent objects, as well as the existence of colimits of algebras was related to reflexive coequalizers in 1-dimension.

It is well known that, for an ordinary monad, any algebra is the reflexive coequalizer of a diagram of free algebras in the category of algebra, called the
bar construction
. Here we recall the corresponding statement for pseudo-algebras, which is established in
[
Bou10
]
[Remark 6.7], and also in
[
LCMV02
]
by a combination of three results (
[
LCMV02
]
[lemma 2.3, proposition 3.2 and corollary 3.3]). Observe that, while
[
LCMV02
]
speaks of
pseudocoequalizer
of codescent objects, the universal property they use is the same as our bicoequalizers as they only require an equivalence of categories in their definition 2.1.

Proposition 2.8

(Bar construction at a pseudo-algbera)
.

Let
(A,a,(\alpha^{s},\alpha^{t}))
be a pseudo-algebra. Then the following diagram, which we will refer as
the bar construction at
(A,a,(\alpha^{s},\alpha^{t}))
, is a codescent object in
T\mbox{-}{\bf psAlg}
, which we will denote
\mathscr{X}_{(A,a,(\alpha^{s},\alpha^{t}))}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTTA,\mu_{TTA},(\xi_{TTA},\rho_{TTA}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTA,\mu_{TA},(\xi_{TA},\rho_{TA}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TA,\mu_{A},(\xi_{A},\rho_{A}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Ta,\mu_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mu_{A},\rho_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\eta_{A},\mu_{\eta_{A}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(TTa,\mu_{Ta})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\mu_{A},\mu_{\mu_{A}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mu_{TA},\rho_{TA})}

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
(A,a,(\alpha^{s},\alpha^{t}))
be a pseudo-algebra: then the pseudomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TA,\mu_{A},(\xi_{A},\rho_{A}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A,a,(\alpha^{t},\alpha^{s}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a,\alpha^{s})}

exhibits
(A,a,(\alpha^{t},\alpha^{s}))
as the bicoequalizer of the codescent object
\mathscr{X}_{(A,a,(\alpha^{s},\alpha^{t}))}
in
T\mbox{-}{\bf psAlg}
. Moreover this bicoequalizer is preserved by the forgetful functor
U_{T}
.

Remark 2.10
.

The bar construction provides an instance of a bicoequalizer which always exists at the level of the 2-category of pseudo-algebras, without assumption about their existence for arbitrary codescent diagrams;l: those latter might indeed not exist in general - and we are going to see what happens when they do in the next section.

2.11
.

We are also going to use the 2-functoriality of both the bar construction and the construction of bicoequalizers. For any morphism of pseudo-algebras
(f,\phi):(A,a,(\alpha^{t},\alpha^{s}))\rightarrow(B,b,(\beta^{t},\beta^{s}))
, we have a morphism of codescent object whose pseudonaturality data are given as the following transformations of pseudomorphisms:

-

at the level of the two lower projections, take the following two squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTA,\mu_{TA},(\xi_{TA},\rho_{TA}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTB,\mu_{TB},(\xi_{TB},\rho_{TB}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TA,\mu_{A},(\xi_{A},\rho_{A}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TB,\mu_{B},(\xi_{B},\rho_{B}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Tf,\mu_{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Ta,\mu_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(TTf,\mu_{Tf})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Tb,\mu_{b})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\phi\atop\simeq}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTA,\mu_{TA},(\xi_{TA},\rho_{TA}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTB,\mu_{TB},(\xi_{TB},\rho_{TB}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TA,\mu_{A},(\xi_{A},\rho_{A}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TB,\mu_{B},(\xi_{B},\rho_{B}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Tf,\mu_{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mu_{A},\rho_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(TTf,\mu_{Tf})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mu_{B},\rho_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{f}\atop\simeq}

-

at the level of their common pseudosection take

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTA,\mu_{TA},(\xi_{TA},\rho_{TA}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTB,\mu_{TB},(\xi_{TB},\rho_{TB}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TA,\mu_{A},(\xi_{A},\rho_{A}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TB,\mu_{B},(\xi_{B},\rho_{B}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Tf,\mu_{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\eta_{A},\mu_{\eta_{A}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(TTf,\mu_{Tf})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\eta_{B},\mu_{\eta_{B}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\eta_{f}\atop\simeq}

-

at the level of the higher codescent data take

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTTA,\mu_{TTA},(\xi_{TTA},\rho_{TTA}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTTB,\mu_{TTB},(\xi_{TTB},\rho_{TTB}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTA,\mu_{TA},(\xi_{TA},\rho_{TA}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTB,\mu_{TB},(\xi_{TB},\rho_{TB}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(TTf,\mu_{T}f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(TTa,\mu_{Ta})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(TTTf,\mu_{TTf})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(TTb,\mu_{Tb})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TT\phi\atop\simeq}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTTA,\mu_{TTA},(\xi_{TTA},\rho_{TTA}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTTB,\mu_{TTB},(\xi_{TTB},\rho_{TTB}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTA,\mu_{TA},(\xi_{TA},\rho_{TA}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTB,\mu_{TB},(\xi_{TB},\rho_{TB}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(TTf,\mu_{T}f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\mu_{A},\mu_{\mu_{A}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(TTTf,\mu_{TTf})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\mu_{B},\mu_{\mu_{B}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\mu_{f}\atop\simeq}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTTA,\mu_{TTA},(\xi_{TTA},\rho_{TTA}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTTB,\mu_{TTB},(\xi_{TTB},\rho_{TTB}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTA,\mu_{TA},(\xi_{TA},\rho_{TA}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TTB,\mu_{TB},(\xi_{TB},\rho_{TB}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(TTf,\mu_{T}f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mu_{TA},\rho_{TA})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(TTTf,\mu_{TTf})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mu_{TB},\rho_{TB})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{Tf}\atop\simeq}

Those data define altogether a pseudonatural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{X}_{(A,a,(\alpha^{t},\alpha^{s}))}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{X}_{(B,b,(\beta^{t},\beta^{s}))}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{(f,\phi)}}

Lemma 2.12
.

A pseudomorphism
(f,\phi)
can be recovered as the morphism induced between the bicoequalizers of the bar construction, that is one can take
(f,\phi)\simeq{\textup{bicoeq}}({\overline{(f,\phi)}})
.

Proof.

This induces uniquely, up to a unique invertible 2-cell, a pseudomorphism between the bicoequalizer of the corresponding bar constructions: but we know those latter are exactly the underlying pseudo-algebras, and the existence of the following transformation of pseudomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TA,\mu_{A},(\xi_{A},\rho_{A}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TB,\mu_{B},(\xi_{B},\rho_{B}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A,a,(\alpha^{t},\alpha^{s}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(B,b,(\beta^{t},\beta^{s}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a,\alpha^{s})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Tf,\mu_{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(b,\beta^{s})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\atop\simeq}

forces the existence, by the universal property of bicoequalizers, of an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicoeq}}(\mathscr{X}_{(A,a,(\alpha^{t},\alpha^{s}))})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicoeq}}(\mathscr{X}_{(B,b,(\beta^{t},\beta^{s}))})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A,a,(\alpha^{t},\alpha^{s}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(B,b,(\beta^{t},\beta^{s}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{bicoeq}}(\overline{(f,\phi)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

This ensures that
(f,\phi)
can be chosen as the morphism induced between the bicoequalizers by pseudofunctoriality of those latter.
∎

3
Oplax bicolimits of pseudo-algebras

In this section, we establish the analog of a well known theorem of monad theory, stating that existence of colimits of algebras amounts to existence of coequalizers of algebras. This result first constructs coproducts as coequalizers of free algebras in the category of algebras: then, for arbitrary colimits can be constructed as coequalizers of parallel pairs between coproducts, existence of coequalizers appears sufficient to generate arbitrary colimits. Here we process in a very similar manner, observing that the coequalizers are replaced everywhere by bicoequalizers of codescent objects. We first construct the oplax bicolimit of a diagram of pseudo-algebras as the bicoequalizer of a codescent object made of free pseudo-algebras. Then, invoking the observation of the first section ensuring that any bicolimit can be constructed from oplax bicolimit and bicoequalizer of codescent objects, we reduce bicocompleteness of the 2-category of pseudo-algebras to existence of bicoequalizer of codescent objects. The overall strategy of this section is close to
[
Bor94
]
[4.3], although one must not only handle coherence conditions attached to the pseudo-algebraic structure but also produce further higher codescent data with intricate coherence conditions that have no equivalent in the 1-dimensional case.

In this section we fix a pseudomonad
(T,\eta,\mu,(\xi,\zeta,\rho))
on a bicocomplete 2-category
\mathcal{C}
.

3.1
Oplax colimit inclusions and structural maps.

Let be
\mathbb{A}:I\rightarrow T\mbox{-}{\bf psAlg}
with
\mathbb{A}(i)=(A_{i},a_{i},(\alpha_{i}^{t},\alpha_{i}^{s}))
and
\mathbb{A}(d)=(f_{d},\phi_{d})
. First compute the oplax bicolimit in
\mathcal{C}
of the underlying diagram
U_{T}\mathbb{A}:I\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
:

with
q_{d}:q_{j}f_{d}\Rightarrow q_{i}
the oplax inclusion 2-cell at
d:i\rightarrow j
. Similarly compute the oplax bicolimit in
\mathcal{C}

(q^{T}_{i}:TA_{i}\rightarrow{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}_{I}TA_{i})_{i\in I}
with

the oplax inclusion 2-cell at

. Then we have an oplax cocone
(q_{i}a_{i}:TA_{i}\rightarrow{\textup{oplaxbicolim}}_{I}TA_{i}

in

given as the pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TA_{i}}}

together with a family of invertible 2-cells
(\theta_{i})_{i\in I}
as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TA_{i}}}
